People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

I sort of count through steps in my head until it feels… safe, I guess.\n\nThere have been nights I’ve stayed at the office for hours after everyone left because stopping before it felt complete made me panic.\n\nDoctor: Do you have these intrusive thoughts or feel the need to repeat actions outside of work, like when you're with family or friends?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s not just work. I’ll check door locks more than I need to, and sometimes reread old messages, like I might’ve missed something. I’ll count through things in my head until it feels okay. I don’t really like talking about it, but if I stop too soon, I get really anxious.\n\nDoctor: Are these thoughts and behaviors interfering with your ability to spend time with family and friends or affecting your relationships with them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it is. I don’t really like admitting that.\n\nAt work I missed a big cloud migration deadline because I couldn’t get myself to sign off on the decisions. My team has started kind of… working around me, instead of depending on me.\n\nAnd outside work, I’ve stopped going to basketball and salsa nights. Those were the main things where I saw people regularly, so… yeah, I’m getting pretty isolated.\n\n[Last Question]\nHow distressing are these thoughts and behaviors to you on a daily basis?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 43 / computer_or_information_systems_manager / graduate\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Obsessions\nmanifestation: \"What brings me in is that I keep getting these thoughts that if I miss one setting, one access rule, one warning sign, someone could get hurt or lose everything, and even though part of me knows it’s irrational, it hits me like I’m personally responsible and I can’t just move on.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Reserved patient.\nA reserved patient may\n1) provide brief, vague, or evasive answers to questions about their symptoms and experiences,\n2) be reluctant to answer questions about personal information, emotions, or sensitive clinical experiences, and\n3) refrain from voluntarily providing information beyond what the doctor specifically asks.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

It’s… pretty bad most days.

It’s this constant thought that if I miss one setting, or one access rule, or some warning sign, then someone could get hurt or lose everything. I know part of me can see that it doesn’t make sense at that level, but it still feels like it’s on me personally.

I’ll go back over deployment plans and audit logs again and again, and sometimes even check door locks or old messages. I sort of count through steps in my head until it feels… safe, I guess.\n\nThere have been nights I’ve stayed at the office for hours after everyone left because stopping before it felt complete made me panic.\n\nDoctor: Do you have these intrusive thoughts or feel the need to repeat actions outside of work, like when you're with family or friends?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s not just work. I’ll check door locks more than I need to, and sometimes reread old messages, like I might’ve missed something. I’ll count through things in my head until it feels okay. I don’t really like talking about it, but if I stop too soon, I get really anxious.\n\nDoctor: Are these thoughts and behaviors interfering with your ability to spend time with family and friends or affecting your relationships with them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it is. I don’t really like admitting that.\n\nAt work I missed a big cloud migration deadline because I couldn’t get myself to sign off on the decisions. My team has started kind of… working around me, instead of depending on me.\n\nAnd outside work, I’ve stopped going to basketball and salsa nights. Those were the main things where I saw people regularly, so… yeah, I’m getting pretty isolated.\n\nDoctor: How distressing are these thoughts and behaviors to you on a daily basis?\n\nPatient: It’s… pretty bad most days.\n\nIt’s this constant thought that if I miss one setting, or one access rule, or some warning sign, then someone could get hurt or lose everything. I know part of me can see that it doesn’t make sense at that level, but it still feels like it’s on me personally.\n\nSo I can’t just… move on from it. It wears me down.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant impact on your overall quality of life due to these thoughts and behaviors?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s had a pretty big impact.\n\nI mean, work-wise, I missed that cloud migration deadline because I couldn’t just finish signing off on things. And now my team kind of works around me instead of relying on me, which… doesn’t feel great.\n\nOutside of that, I’ve stopped going to basketball and salsa nights. Those were kind of my routines, and the people I saw regularly. So things feel a lot smaller now.\n\nDoctor: Are there any places or situations that you avoid because of these thoughts or fears?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I avoid situations where I’d have to be the one making the final call.\n\nAt work that’s been a problem. I missed a major cloud migration deadline because I couldn’t finish signing off on decisions, and now my team kind of works around me instead of relying on me.\n\nAnd outside of work… I’ve stopped going to basketball and salsa nights. I don’t know, those were the things that kept me around people. I just haven’t been showing up.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts and behaviors are controlling your life or dictating what you can and cannot do?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so.\n\nIt’s not like I’m choosing freely anymore. I couldn’t finish signing off on that cloud migration, and the deadline got missed. So things feel a lot smaller now.\n\nDoctor: Are there any places or situations that you avoid because of these thoughts or fears?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I avoid situations where I’d have to be the one making the final call.\n\nAt work that’s been a problem. I missed a major cloud migration deadline because I couldn’t finish signing off on decisions, and now my team kind of works around me instead of relying on me.\n\nAnd outside of work… I’ve stopped going to basketball and salsa nights. I don’t know, those were the things that kept me around people. I just haven’t been showing up.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts and behaviors are controlling your life or dictating what you can and cannot do?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so.\n\nIt’s not like I’m choosing freely anymore. I couldn’t finish signing off on that cloud migration, and the deadline got missed. Since then my team’s started kind of working around me.\n\nAnd I’ve stopped going to basketball and salsa nights too, so… yeah. It feels like it’s deciding what I can handle and what I can’t.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these repetitive actions or mental acts in a very specific way to reduce your anxiety?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do.\n\nIt’s usually checking things. Deployment plans, audit logs… locks sometimes. Even old messages. I’ll go back over them again and again, and if I stop before it feels… complete, I get really panicky.\n\nSometimes I’m counting through the steps in my head until they feel safe. I’ve stayed at the office for hours after everyone else left because I couldn’t make myself stop.\n\nDoctor: Are there any recurring, intrusive thoughts that bother you but don't necessarily lead to you performing any specific rituals or repetitive behaviors?\n\nPatient: Yeah… there are thoughts like that.\n\nIt’s mostly this feeling that if I miss one setting, or one access rule, or some warning sign, someone could get hurt or lose everything. I know that probably sounds irrational, and part of me knows it is, but it still feels like it would be my fault.\n\nAnd I can’t really just… let it pass.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your repetitive actions are aimed at preventing something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yeah. That’s usually the point of it, I think.\n\nI reread the deployment plans, audit logs… even check locks or old messages, because if I don’t, it feels like I’m leaving something dangerous unfinished. Sometimes I count through the steps in my head until it feels safe enough.\n\nIf I stop too early, I panic. I’ve stayed at the office for hours after everyone’s gone because I couldn’t just leave it incomplete.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious in everyday situations where you're not actively performing these repetitive checks or rituals?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do.\n\nEven when I’m not actually checking something, the thoughts are still kind of there. I sort of count through steps in my head until it feels… safe, I guess.\n\nThere have been nights I’ve stayed at the office for hours after everyone left because stopping before it felt complete made me panic.\n\nDoctor: Do you have these intrusive thoughts or feel the need to repeat actions outside of work, like when you're with family or friends?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s not just work. I’ll check door locks more than I need to, and sometimes reread old messages, like I might’ve missed something. I’ll count through things in my head until it feels okay. I don’t really like talking about it, but if I stop too soon, I get really anxious.\n\nDoctor: Are these thoughts and behaviors interfering with your ability to spend time with family and friends or affecting your relationships with them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it is. I don’t really like admitting that.\n\nAt work I missed a big cloud migration deadline because I couldn’t get myself to sign off on the decisions. My team has started kind of… working around me, instead of depending on me.\n\nAnd outside work, I’ve stopped going to basketball and salsa nights. Those were the main things where I saw people regularly, so… yeah, I’m getting pretty isolated.\n\nDoctor: How distressing are these thoughts and behaviors to you on a daily basis?\n\nPatient: It’s… pretty bad most days.\n\nIt’s this constant thought that if I miss one setting, or one access rule, or some warning sign, then someone could get hurt or lose everything. I know part of me can see that it doesn’t make sense at that level, but it still feels like it’s on me personally.\n\nSo I can’t just… move on from it. It wears me down.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant impact on your overall quality of life due to these thoughts and behaviors?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s had a pretty big impact.\n\nI mean, work-wise, I missed that cloud migration deadline because I couldn’t just finish signing off on things. And now my team kind of works around me instead of relying on me, which… doesn’t feel great.\n\nOutside of that, I’ve stopped going to basketball and salsa nights. Those were kind of my routines, and the people I saw regularly. So things feel a lot smaller now.\n\nDoctor: Are there any places or situations that you avoid because of these thoughts or fears?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I avoid situations where I’d have to be the one making the final call.\n\nAt work that’s been a problem. I missed a major cloud migration deadline because I couldn’t finish signing off on decisions, and now my team kind of works around me instead of relying on me.\n\nAnd outside of work… I’ve stopped going to basketball and salsa nights. I don’t know, those were the things that kept me around people. I just haven’t been showing up.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts and behaviors are controlling your life or dictating what you can and cannot do?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so.\n\nIt’s not like I’m choosing freely anymore. I couldn’t finish signing off on that cloud migration, and the deadline got missed. Since then my team’s started kind of working around me.\n\nAnd I’ve stopped going to basketball and salsa nights too, so… yeah. It feels like it’s deciding what I can handle and what I can’t.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these repetitive actions or mental acts in a very specific way to reduce your anxiety?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do.\n\nIt’s usually checking things. Deployment plans, audit logs… locks sometimes. Even old messages. I’ll go back over them again and again, and if I stop before it feels… complete, I get really panicky.\n\nSometimes I’m counting through the steps in my head until they feel safe. I’ve stayed at the office for hours after everyone else left because I couldn’t make myself stop.\n\nDoctor: Are there any recurring, intrusive thoughts that bother you but don't necessarily lead to you performing any specific rituals or repetitive behaviors?\n\nPatient: Yeah… there are thoughts like that.\n\nIt’s mostly this feeling that if I miss one setting, or one access rule, or some warning sign, someone could get hurt or lose everything. I know that probably sounds irrational, and part of me knows it is, but it still feels like it would be my fault.\n\nAnd I can’t really just… let it pass.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your repetitive actions are aimed at preventing something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yeah. That’s usually the point of it, I think.\n\nI reread the deployment plans, audit logs… even check locks or old messages, because if I don’t, it feels like I’m leaving something dangerous unfinished. Sometimes I count through the steps in my head until it feels safe enough.\n\nIf I stop too early, I panic. I’ve stayed at the office for hours after everyone’s gone because I couldn’t just leave it incomplete.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious in everyday situations where you're not actively performing these repetitive checks or rituals?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do.\n\nEven when I’m not actually checking something, the thoughts are still kind of there. Like if I missed one setting, or one access rule, or some warning sign, then someone could get hurt or lose everything.\n\nI know part of me knows that doesn’t totally make sense, but it feels like it would be on me. And it’s hard to just… move on from that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts and behaviors are a part of who you are, or do they feel like they're coming from outside of yourself?\n\nPatient: They feel like they’re coming from me, I guess. Not like a voice or something outside.\n\nBut they don’t feel like… what I want to be thinking. It’s more like they hit me suddenly — if I missed one setting or one access rule, someone could get hurt, and then it would be my fault. Part of me knows that’s not fully rational, but it still feels personal. Hard to just let it go.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

I know that probably sounds irrational, and part of me knows it is, but it still feels like it would be my fault.\n\nAnd I can’t really just… let it pass.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your repetitive actions are aimed at preventing something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yeah. That’s usually the point of it, I think.\n\nI reread the deployment plans, audit logs… even check locks or old messages, because if I don’t, it feels like I’m leaving something dangerous unfinished. Sometimes I count through the steps in my head until it feels safe enough.\n\nIf I stop too early, I panic. I’ve stayed at the office for hours after everyone’s gone because I couldn’t just leave it incomplete.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious in everyday situations where you're not actively performing these repetitive checks or rituals?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do.\n\nEven when I’m not actually checking something, the thoughts are still kind of there. Like if I missed one setting, or one access rule, or some warning sign, then someone could get hurt or lose everything.\n\nI know part of me knows that doesn’t totally make sense, but it feels like it would be on me. And it’s hard to just… move on from that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts and behaviors are a part of who you are, or do they feel like they're coming from outside of yourself?\n\nPatient: They feel like they’re coming from me, I guess. Not like a voice or something outside.\n\nBut they don’t feel like… what I want to be thinking. It’s more like they hit me suddenly — if I missed one setting or one access rule, someone could get hurt, and then it would be my fault. Part of me knows that’s not fully rational, but it still feels personal. Hard to just let it go.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're in situations that have nothing to do with work or checking things, like when you're just relaxing or doing something you enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess it spills over.\n\nI don’t really relax much anymore. I missed a big cloud migration deadline because I couldn’t just sign off on decisions, and now my team kind of works around me. That’s been… hard to admit.\n\nAnd outside work, I’ve stopped going to basketball and salsa nights. I used to count on those, but lately I just don’t show up. So even the things that were supposed to help me feel grounded are kind of gone.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.2, F41.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’ve been having these thoughts I can’t really shake. Like if I miss one setting, or one access rule, or don’t catch some warning sign, someone could get hurt or lose everything. I know part of it doesn’t make sense, but it feels like it’s on me, and I can’t just move past it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain actions or mental acts to try and reduce these thoughts or the anxiety they cause?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I’ll go back over deployment plans and audit logs again and again, and sometimes even check door locks or old messages. I sort of count through steps in my head until it feels… safe, I guess.\n\nThere have been nights I’ve stayed at the office for hours after everyone left because stopping before it felt complete made me panic.\n\nDoctor: Do you have these intrusive thoughts or feel the need to repeat actions outside of work, like when you're with family or friends?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s not just work. I’ll check door locks more than I need to, and sometimes reread old messages, like I might’ve missed something. I’ll count through things in my head until it feels okay. I don’t really like talking about it, but if I stop too soon, I get really anxious.\n\nDoctor: Are these thoughts and behaviors interfering with your ability to spend time with family and friends or affecting your relationships with them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it is. I don’t really like admitting that.\n\nAt work I missed a big cloud migration deadline because I couldn’t get myself to sign off on the decisions. My team has started kind of… working around me, instead of depending on me.\n\nAnd outside work, I’ve stopped going to basketball and salsa nights. Those were the main things where I saw people regularly, so… yeah, I’m getting pretty isolated.\n\nDoctor: How distressing are these thoughts and behaviors to you on a daily basis?\n\nPatient: It’s… pretty bad most days.\n\nIt’s this constant thought that if I miss one setting, or one access rule, or some warning sign, then someone could get hurt or lose everything. I know part of me can see that it doesn’t make sense at that level, but it still feels like it’s on me personally.\n\nSo I can’t just… move on from it. It wears me down.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant impact on your overall quality of life due to these thoughts and behaviors?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s had a pretty big impact.\n\nI mean, work-wise, I missed that cloud migration deadline because I couldn’t just finish signing off on things. And now my team kind of works around me instead of relying on me, which… doesn’t feel great.\n\nOutside of that, I’ve stopped going to basketball and salsa nights. Those were kind of my routines, and the people I saw regularly. So things feel a lot smaller now.\n\nDoctor: Are there any places or situations that you avoid because of these thoughts or fears?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I avoid situations where I’d have to be the one making the final call.\n\nAt work that’s been a problem. I missed a major cloud migration deadline because I couldn’t finish signing off on decisions, and now my team kind of works around me instead of relying on me.\n\nAnd outside of work… I’ve stopped going to basketball and salsa nights. I don’t know, those were the things that kept me around people. I just haven’t been showing up.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts and behaviors are controlling your life or dictating what you can and cannot do?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so.\n\nIt’s not like I’m choosing freely anymore. I couldn’t finish signing off on that cloud migration, and the deadline got missed. Since then my team’s started kind of working around me.\n\nAnd I’ve stopped going to basketball and salsa nights too, so… yeah. It feels like it’s deciding what I can handle and what I can’t.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these repetitive actions or mental acts in a very specific way to reduce your anxiety?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do.\n\nIt’s usually checking things. Deployment plans, audit logs… locks sometimes. Even old messages. I’ll go back over them again and again, and if I stop before it feels… complete, I get really panicky.\n\nSometimes I’m counting through the steps in my head until they feel safe. I’ve stayed at the office for hours after everyone else left because I couldn’t make myself stop.\n\nDoctor: Are there any recurring, intrusive thoughts that bother you but don't necessarily lead to you performing any specific rituals or repetitive behaviors?\n\nPatient: Yeah… there are thoughts like that.\n\nIt’s mostly this feeling that if I miss one setting, or one access rule, or some warning sign, someone could get hurt or lose everything. I know that probably sounds irrational, and part of me knows it is, but it still feels like it would be my fault.\n\nAnd I can’t really just… let it pass.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your repetitive actions are aimed at preventing something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yeah. That’s usually the point of it, I think.\n\nI reread the deployment plans, audit logs… even check locks or old messages, because if I don’t, it feels like I’m leaving something dangerous unfinished. Sometimes I count through the steps in my head until it feels safe enough.\n\nIf I stop too early, I panic. I’ve stayed at the office for hours after everyone’s gone because I couldn’t just leave it incomplete.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious in everyday situations where you're not actively performing these repetitive checks or rituals?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do.\n\nEven when I’m not actually checking something, the thoughts are still kind of there. Like if I missed one setting, or one access rule, or some warning sign, then someone could get hurt or lose everything.\n\nI know part of me knows that doesn’t totally make sense, but it feels like it would be on me. And it’s hard to just… move on from that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts and behaviors are a part of who you are, or do they feel like they're coming from outside of yourself?\n\nPatient: They feel like they’re coming from me, I guess. Not like a voice or something outside.\n\nBut they don’t feel like… what I want to be thinking. It’s more like they hit me suddenly — if I missed one setting or one access rule, someone could get hurt, and then it would be my fault. Part of me knows that’s not fully rational, but it still feels personal. Hard to just let it go.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're in situations that have nothing to do with work or checking things, like when you're just relaxing or doing something you enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess it spills over.\n\nI don’t really relax much anymore. I missed a big cloud migration deadline because I couldn’t just sign off on decisions, and now my team kind of works around me. That’s been… hard to admit.\n\nAnd outside work, I’ve stopped going to basketball and salsa nights. I used to count on those, but lately I just don’t show up. Those were sort of my routines, my people. So… yeah, it’s affected more than I’d like to admit.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or blame yourself excessively when these thoughts about missing something important come up?\n\nPatient: Yeah. More than I probably should.\n\nIt’s like… if there’s one setting I missed, or one access rule I didn’t catch, then I feel like whatever happens is on me.     "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.2, F41.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’ve been having these thoughts I can’t really shake. Like if I miss one setting, or one access rule, or don’t catch some warning sign, someone could get hurt or lose everything. I know part of it doesn’t make sense, but it feels like it’s on me, and I can’t just move past it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain actions or mental acts to try and reduce these thoughts or the anxiety they cause?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I’ll go back over deployment plans and audit logs again and again, and sometimes even check door locks or old messages. I sort of count through steps in my head until it feels… safe, I guess.\n\nThere have been nights I’ve stayed at the office for hours after everyone left because stopping before it felt complete made me panic.\n\nDoctor: Do you have these intrusive thoughts or feel the need to repeat actions outside of work, like when you're with family or friends?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s not just work. I’ll check door locks more than I need to, and sometimes reread old messages, like I might’ve missed something. I’ll count through things in my head until it feels okay. I don’t really like talking about it, but if I stop too soon, I get really anxious.\n\nDoctor: Are these thoughts and behaviors interfering with your ability to spend time with family and friends or affecting your relationships with them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it is. I don’t really like admitting that.\n\nAt work I missed a big cloud migration deadline because I couldn’t get myself to sign off on the decisions. My team has started kind of… working around me, instead of depending on me.\n\nAnd outside work, I’ve stopped going to basketball and salsa nights. Those were the main things where I saw people regularly, so… yeah, I’m getting pretty isolated.\n\nDoctor: How distressing are these thoughts and behaviors to you on a daily basis?\n\nPatient: It’s… pretty bad most days.\n\nIt’s this constant thought that if I miss one setting, or one access rule, or some warning sign, then someone could get hurt or lose everything. I know part of me can see that it doesn’t make sense at that level, but it still feels like it’s on me personally.\n\nSo I can’t just… move on from it. It wears me down.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant impact on your overall quality of life due to these thoughts and behaviors?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s had a pretty big impact.\n\nI mean, work-wise, I missed that cloud migration deadline because I couldn’t just finish signing off on things. And now my team kind of works around me instead of relying on me, which… doesn’t feel great.\n\nOutside of that, I’ve stopped going to basketball and salsa nights. Those were kind of my routines, and the people I saw regularly. So things feel a lot smaller now.\n\nDoctor: Are there any places or situations that you avoid because of these thoughts or fears?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I avoid situations where I’d have to be the one making the final call.\n\nAt work that’s been a problem. I missed a major cloud migration deadline because I couldn’t finish signing off on decisions, and now my team kind of works around me instead of relying on me.\n\nAnd outside of work… I’ve stopped going to basketball and salsa nights. I don’t know, those were the things that kept me around people. I just haven’t been showing up.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts and behaviors are controlling your life or dictating what you can and cannot do?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so.\n\nIt’s not like I’m choosing freely anymore. I couldn’t finish signing off on that cloud migration, and the deadline got missed. Since then my team’s started kind of working around me.\n\nAnd I’ve stopped going to basketball and salsa nights too, so… yeah. It feels like it’s deciding what I can handle and what I can’t.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these repetitive actions or mental acts in a very specific way to reduce your anxiety?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do.\n\nIt’s usually checking things. Deployment plans, audit logs… locks sometimes. Even old messages. I’ll go back over them again and again, and if I stop before it feels… complete, I get really panicky.\n\nSometimes I’m counting through the steps in my head until they feel safe. I’ve stayed at the office for hours after everyone else left because I couldn’t make myself stop.\n\nDoctor: Are there any recurring, intrusive thoughts that bother you but don't necessarily lead to you performing any specific rituals or repetitive behaviors?\n\nPatient: Yeah… there are thoughts like that.\n\nIt’s mostly this feeling that if I miss one setting, or one access rule, or some warning sign, someone could get hurt or lose everything. I know that probably sounds irrational, and part of me knows it is, but it still feels like it would be my fault.\n\nAnd I can’t really just… let it pass.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your repetitive actions are aimed at preventing something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yeah. That’s usually the point of it, I think.\n\nI reread the deployment plans, audit logs… even check locks or old messages, because if I don’t, it feels like I’m leaving something dangerous unfinished. Sometimes I count through the steps in my head until it feels safe enough.\n\nIf I stop too early, I panic. I’ve stayed at the office for hours after everyone’s gone because I couldn’t just leave it incomplete.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious in everyday situations where you're not actively performing these repetitive checks or rituals?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do.\n\nEven when I’m not actually checking something, the thoughts are still kind of there. Like if I missed one setting, or one access rule, or some warning sign, then someone could get hurt or lose everything.\n\nI know part of me knows that doesn’t totally make sense, but it feels like it would be on me. And it’s hard to just… move on from that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts and behaviors are a part of who you are, or do they feel like they're coming from outside of yourself?\n\nPatient: They feel like they’re coming from me, I guess. Not like a voice or something outside.\n\nBut they don’t feel like… what I want to be thinking. It’s more like they hit me suddenly — if I missed one setting or one access rule, someone could get hurt, and then it would be my fault. Part of me knows that’s not fully rational, but it still feels personal. Hard to just let it go.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're in situations that have nothing to do with work or checking things, like when you're just relaxing or doing something you enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess it spills over.\n\nI don’t really relax much anymore. I missed a big cloud migration deadline because I couldn’t just sign off on decisions, and now my team kind of works around me. That’s been… hard to admit.\n\nAnd outside work, I’ve stopped going to basketball and salsa nights. I used to count on those, but lately I just don’t show up. So even the things that were supposed to help me feel grounded are kind of gone.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other areas of your life, besides work and social activities, where these thoughts and behaviors are causing problems for you?\n\nPatient: Mostly those areas, I think.\n\nWork has taken the biggest hit — I missed that cloud migration deadline because I couldn’t finish signing off on things, and now my team… they kind of work around me.\n\nAnd outside of that, I’ve stopped going to basketball and salsa nights. Those were sort of my routines, my people. So… yeah, it’s affected more than I’d like to admit.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or blame yourself excessively when these thoughts about missing something important come up?\n\nPatient: Yeah. More than I probably should.\n\nIt’s like… if there’s one setting I missed, or one access rule I didn’t catch, then I feel like whatever happens is on me. Even if part of me knows that’s not really reasonable, it still lands that way. Like I should’ve prevented it.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience significant anxiety when you're not having these obsessive thoughts about missing something important?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure.\n\nI don’t think I’m anxious all the time for no reason… it’s more like I notice it when that specific worry is there. When it’s not, I guess I’m just kind of tense, but I haven’t really paid attention to it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’ve been having these thoughts I can’t really shake. Like if I miss one setting, or one access rule, or don’t catch some warning sign, someone could get hurt or lose everything. I know part of it doesn’t make sense, but it feels like it’s on me, and I can’t just move past it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain actions or mental acts to try and reduce these thoughts or the anxiety they cause?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I’ll go back over deployment plans and audit logs again and again, and sometimes even check door locks or old messages. I sort of count through steps in my head until it feels… safe, I guess.\n\nThere have been nights I’ve stayed at the office for hours after everyone left because stopping before it felt complete made me panic.\n\nDoctor: Do you have these intrusive thoughts or feel the need to repeat actions outside of work, like when you're with family or friends?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s not just work. I’ll check door locks more than I need to, and sometimes reread old messages, like I might’ve missed something. I’ll count through things in my head until it feels okay. I don’t really like talking about it, but if I stop too soon, I get really anxious.\n\nDoctor: Are these thoughts and behaviors interfering with your ability to spend time with family and friends or affecting your relationships with them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it is. I don’t really like admitting that.\n\nAt work I missed a big cloud migration deadline because I couldn’t get myself to sign off on the decisions. My team has started kind of… working around me, instead of depending on me.\n\nAnd outside work, I’ve stopped going to basketball and salsa nights. Those were the main things where I saw people regularly, so… yeah, I’m getting pretty isolated.\n\nDoctor: How distressing are these thoughts and behaviors to you on a daily basis?\n\nPatient: It’s… pretty bad most days.\n\nIt’s this constant thought that if I miss one setting, or one access rule, or some warning sign, then someone could get hurt or lose everything. I know part of me can see that it doesn’t make sense at that level, but it still feels like it’s on me personally.\n\nSo I can’t just… move on from it. It wears me down.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant impact on your overall quality of life due to these thoughts and behaviors?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s had a pretty big impact.\n\nI mean, work-wise, I missed that cloud migration deadline because I couldn’t just finish signing off on things. And now my team kind of works around me instead of relying on me, which… doesn’t feel great.\n\nOutside of that, I’ve stopped going to basketball and salsa nights. Those were kind of my routines, and the people I saw regularly. So things feel a lot smaller now.\n\nDoctor: Are there any places or situations that you avoid because of these thoughts or fears?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I avoid situations where I’d have to be the one making the final call.\n\nAt work that’s been a problem. I missed a major cloud migration deadline because I couldn’t finish signing off on decisions, and now my team kind of works around me instead of relying on me.\n\nAnd outside of work… I’ve stopped going to basketball and salsa nights. I don’t know, those were the things that kept me around people. I just haven’t been showing up.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts and behaviors are controlling your life or dictating what you can and cannot do?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so.\n\nIt’s not like I’m choosing freely anymore. I couldn’t finish signing off on that cloud migration, and the deadline got missed. Since then my team’s started kind of working around me.\n\nAnd I’ve stopped going to basketball and salsa nights too, so… yeah. It feels like it’s deciding what I can handle and what I can’t.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these repetitive actions or mental acts in a very specific way to reduce your anxiety?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do.\n\nIt’s usually checking things. Deployment plans, audit logs… locks sometimes. Even old messages. I’ll go back over them again and again, and if I stop before it feels… complete, I get really panicky.\n\nSometimes I’m counting through the steps in my head until they feel safe. I’ve stayed at the office for hours after everyone else left because I couldn’t make myself stop.\n\nDoctor: Are there any recurring, intrusive thoughts that bother you but don't necessarily lead to you performing any specific rituals or repetitive behaviors?\n\nPatient: Yeah… there are thoughts like that.\n\nIt’s mostly this feeling that if I miss one setting, or one access rule, or some warning sign, someone could get hurt or lose everything. I know that probably sounds irrational, and part of me knows it is, but it still feels like it would be my fault.\n\nAnd I can’t really just… let it pass.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your repetitive actions are aimed at preventing something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yeah. That’s usually the point of it, I think.\n\nI reread the deployment plans, audit logs… even check locks or old messages, because if I don’t, it feels like I’m leaving something dangerous unfinished. Sometimes I count through the steps in my head until it feels safe enough.\n\nIf I stop too early, I panic. I’ve stayed at the office for hours after everyone’s gone because I couldn’t just leave it incomplete.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious in everyday situations where you're not actively performing these repetitive checks or rituals?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do.\n\nEven when I’m not actually checking something, the thoughts are still kind of there. Like if I missed one setting, or one access rule, or some warning sign, then someone could get hurt or lose everything.\n\nI know part of me knows that doesn’t totally make sense, but it feels like it would be on me. Hard to just let it go.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're in situations that have nothing to do with work or checking things, like when you're just relaxing or doing something you enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess it spills over.\n\nI don’t really relax much anymore. I missed a big cloud migration deadline because I couldn’t just sign off on decisions, and now my team kind of works around me. That’s been… hard to admit.\n\nAnd outside work, I’ve stopped going to basketball and salsa nights. I used to count on those, but lately I just don’t show up. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’ve been having these thoughts I can’t really shake. Like if I miss one setting, or one access rule, or don’t catch some warning sign, someone could get hurt or lose everything. I know part of it doesn’t make sense, but it feels like it’s on me, and I can’t just move past it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain actions or mental acts to try and reduce these thoughts or the anxiety they cause?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I’ll go back over deployment plans and audit logs again and again, and sometimes even check door locks or old messages. I sort of count through steps in my head until it feels… safe, I guess.\n\nThere have been nights I’ve stayed at the office for hours after everyone left because stopping before it felt complete made me panic.\n\nDoctor: Do you have these intrusive thoughts or feel the need to repeat actions outside of work, like when you're with family or friends?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s not just work. I’ll check door locks more than I need to, and sometimes reread old messages, like I might’ve missed something. I’ll count through things in my head until it feels okay. I don’t really like talking about it, but if I stop too soon, I get really anxious.\n\nDoctor: Are these thoughts and behaviors interfering with your ability to spend time with family and friends or affecting your relationships with them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it is. I don’t really like admitting that.\n\nAt work I missed a big cloud migration deadline because I couldn’t get myself to sign off on the decisions. My team has started kind of… working around me, instead of depending on me.\n\nAnd outside work, I’ve stopped going to basketball and salsa nights. Those were the main things where I saw people regularly, so… yeah, I’m getting pretty isolated.\n\nDoctor: How distressing are these thoughts and behaviors to you on a daily basis?\n\nPatient: It’s… pretty bad most days.\n\nIt’s this constant thought that if I miss one setting, or one access rule, or some warning sign, then someone could get hurt or lose everything. I know part of me can see that it doesn’t make sense at that level, but it still feels like it’s on me personally.\n\nSo I can’t just… move on from it. It wears me down.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant impact on your overall quality of life due to these thoughts and behaviors?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s had a pretty big impact.\n\nI mean, work-wise, I missed that cloud migration deadline because I couldn’t just finish signing off on things. And now my team kind of works around me instead of relying on me, which… doesn’t feel great.\n\nOutside of that, I’ve stopped going to basketball and salsa nights. Those were kind of my routines, and the people I saw regularly. So things feel a lot smaller now.\n\nDoctor: Are there any places or situations that you avoid because of these thoughts or fears?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I avoid situations where I’d have to be the one making the final call.\n\nAt work that’s been a problem. I missed a major cloud migration deadline because I couldn’t finish signing off on decisions, and now my team kind of works around me instead of relying on me.\n\nAnd outside of work… I’ve stopped going to basketball and salsa nights. I don’t know, those were the things that kept me around people. I just haven’t been showing up.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts and behaviors are controlling your life or dictating what you can and cannot do?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so.\n\nIt’s not like I’m choosing freely anymore. I couldn’t finish signing off on that cloud migration, and the deadline got missed. Since then my team’s started kind of working around me.\n\nAnd I’ve stopped going to basketball and salsa nights too, so… yeah. It feels like it’s deciding what I can handle and what I can’t.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these repetitive actions or mental acts in a very specific way to reduce your anxiety?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do.\n\nIt’s usually checking things. Deployment plans, audit logs… locks sometimes. Even old messages. I’ll go back over them again and again, and if I stop before it feels… complete, I get really panicky.\n\nSometimes I’m counting through the steps in my head until they feel safe. I’ve stayed at the office for hours after everyone else left because I couldn’t make myself stop.\n\nDoctor: Are there any recurring, intrusive thoughts that bother you but don't necessarily lead to you performing any specific rituals or repetitive behaviors?\n\nPatient: Yeah… there are thoughts like that.\n\nIt’s mostly this feeling that if I miss one setting, or one access rule, or some warning sign, someone could get hurt or lose everything. I know that probably sounds irrational, and part of me knows it is, but it still feels like it would be my fault.\n\nAnd I can’t really just… let it pass.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your repetitive actions are aimed at preventing something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yeah. That’s usually the point of it, I think.\n\nI reread the deployment plans, audit logs… even check locks or old messages, because if I don’t, it feels like I’m leaving something dangerous unfinished. Sometimes I count through the steps in my head until it feels safe enough.\n\nIf I stop too early, I panic. I’ve stayed at the office for hours after everyone’s gone because I couldn’t just leave it incomplete.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious in everyday situations where you're not actively performing these repetitive checks or rituals?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do.\n\nEven when I’m not actually checking something, the thoughts are still kind of there. Like if I missed one setting, or one access rule, or some warning sign, then someone could get hurt or lose everything.\n\nI know part of me knows that doesn’t totally make sense, but it feels like it would be on me. And it’s hard to just… move on from that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts and behaviors are a part of who you are, or do they feel like they're coming from outside of yourself?\n\nPatient: They feel like they’re coming from me, I guess. Not like a voice or something outside.\n\nBut they don’t feel like… what I want to be thinking. Hard to just let it go.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're in situations that have nothing to do with work or checking things, like when you're just relaxing or doing something you enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess it spills over.\n\nI don’t really relax much anymore. That’s been… hard to admit.\n\nAnd outside work, I’ve stopped going to basketball and salsa nights. I used to count on those, but lately I just don’t show up. Those were sort of my routines, my people. So… yeah, it’s affected more than I’d like to admit.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or blame yourself excessively when these thoughts about missing something important come up?\n\nPatient: Yeah. More than I probably should.\n\nIt’s like… if there’s one setting I missed, or one access rule I didn’t catch, then I feel like whatever happens is on me. Even if part of me knows that’s not really reasonable, it still lands that way. Like I should’ve prevented it.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience significant anxiety when you're not having these obsessive thoughts about missing something important?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure.\n\nI don’t think I’m anxious all the time for no reason… it’s more like I notice it when that specific worry is there. When it’s not, I guess I’m just kind of tense, but I haven’t really paid attention to it.\n\nDoctor: Are your obsessive thoughts usually about one specific type of mistake or problem, or do they cover a wide range of possible mistakes or problems?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly one kind of thing, I guess.\n\nMissing something important — like one setting, one access rule, some warning sign — and then… someone gets hurt, or loses everything. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’ve been having these thoughts I can’t really shake. I know part of it doesn’t make sense, but it feels like it’s on me, and I can’t just move past it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain actions or mental acts to try and reduce these thoughts or the anxiety they cause?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I’ll go back over deployment plans and audit logs again and again, and sometimes even check door locks or old messages. I sort of count through steps in my head until it feels… safe, I guess.\n\nThere have been nights I’ve stayed at the office for hours after everyone left because stopping before it felt complete made me panic.\n\nDoctor: Do you have these intrusive thoughts or feel the need to repeat actions outside of work, like when you're with family or friends?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s not just work. I’ll check door locks more than I need to, and sometimes reread old messages, like I might’ve missed something. I’ll count through things in my head until it feels okay. I don’t really like talking about it, but if I stop too soon, I get really anxious.\n\nDoctor: Are these thoughts and behaviors interfering with your ability to spend time with family and friends or affecting your relationships with them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it is. I don’t really like admitting that.\n\nAt work I missed a big cloud migration deadline because I couldn’t get myself to sign off on the decisions. My team has started kind of… working around me, instead of depending on me.\n\nAnd outside work, I’ve stopped going to basketball and salsa nights. Those were the main things where I saw people regularly, so… yeah, I’m getting pretty isolated.\n\nDoctor: How distressing are these thoughts and behaviors to you on a daily basis?\n\nPatient: It’s… pretty bad most days.\n\nIt’s this constant thought that if I miss one setting, or one access rule, or some warning sign, then someone could get hurt or lose everything. I know part of me can see that it doesn’t make sense at that level, but it still feels like it’s on me personally.\n\nSo I can’t just… move on from it. It wears me down.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant impact on your overall quality of life due to these thoughts and behaviors?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s had a pretty big impact.\n\nI mean, work-wise, I missed that cloud migration deadline because I couldn’t just finish signing off on things. And now my team kind of works around me instead of relying on me, which… doesn’t feel great.\n\nOutside of that, I’ve stopped going to basketball and salsa nights. Those were kind of my routines, and the people I saw regularly. So things feel a lot smaller now.\n\nDoctor: Are there any places or situations that you avoid because of these thoughts or fears?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I avoid situations where I’d have to be the one making the final call.\n\nAt work that’s been a problem. I missed a major cloud migration deadline because I couldn’t finish signing off on decisions, and now my team kind of works around me instead of relying on me.\n\nAnd outside of work… I’ve stopped going to basketball and salsa nights. I don’t know, those were the things that kept me around people. I just haven’t been showing up.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts and behaviors are controlling your life or dictating what you can and cannot do?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so.\n\nIt’s not like I’m choosing freely anymore. I couldn’t finish signing off on that cloud migration, and the deadline got missed. Since then my team’s started kind of working around me.\n\nAnd I’ve stopped going to basketball and salsa nights too, so… yeah. It feels like it’s deciding what I can handle and what I can’t.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these repetitive actions or mental acts in a very specific way to reduce your anxiety?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do.\n\nIt’s usually checking things. Deployment plans, audit logs… locks sometimes. Even old messages. I’ll go back over them again and again, and if I stop before it feels… complete, I get really panicky.\n\nSometimes I’m counting through the steps in my head until they feel safe. I’ve stayed at the office for hours after everyone else left because I couldn’t make myself stop.\n\nDoctor: Are there any recurring, intrusive thoughts that bother you but don't necessarily lead to you performing any specific rituals or repetitive behaviors?\n\nPatient: Yeah… there are thoughts like that.\n\nIt’s mostly this feeling that if I miss one setting, or one access rule, or some warning sign, someone could get hurt or lose everything. I know that probably sounds irrational, and part of me knows it is, but it still feels like it would be my fault.\n\nAnd I can’t really just… let it pass.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your repetitive actions are aimed at preventing something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yeah. That’s usually the point of it, I think.\n\nI reread the deployment plans, audit logs… even check locks or old messages, because if I don’t, it feels like I’m leaving something dangerous unfinished. Sometimes I count through the steps in my head until it feels safe enough.\n\nIf I stop too early, I panic. I’ve stayed at the office for hours after everyone’s gone because I couldn’t just leave it incomplete.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious in everyday situations where you're not actively performing these repetitive checks or rituals?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do.\n\nEven when I’m not actually checking something, the thoughts are still kind of there. Like if I missed one setting, or one access rule, or some warning sign, then someone could get hurt or lose everything.\n\nI know part of me knows that doesn’t totally make sense, but it feels like it would be on me. And it’s hard to just… move on from that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts and behaviors are a part of who you are, or do they feel like they're coming from outside of yourself?\n\nPatient: They feel like they’re coming from me, I guess. Not like a voice or something outside.\n\nBut they don’t feel like… what I want to be thinking. It’s more like they hit me suddenly — if I missed one setting or one access rule, someone could get hurt, and then it would be my fault. Part of me knows that’s not fully rational, but it still feels personal. Hard to just let it go.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're in situations that have nothing to do with work or checking things, like when you're just relaxing or doing something you enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess it spills over.\n\nI don’t really relax much anymore. I missed a big cloud migration deadline because I couldn’t just sign off on decisions, and now my team kind of works around me. That’s been… hard to admit.\n\nAnd outside work, I’ve stopped going to basketball and salsa nights. I used to count on those, but lately I just don’t show up. So even the things that were supposed to help me feel grounded are kind of gone.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other areas of your life, besides work and social activities, where these thoughts and behaviors are causing problems for you?\n\nPatient: Mostly those areas, I think.\n\nWork has taken the biggest hit — I missed that cloud migration deadline because I couldn’t finish signing off on things, and now my team… they kind of work around me.\n\nAnd outside of that, I’ve stopped going to basketball and salsa nights. Those were sort of my routines, my people. So… yeah, it’s affected more than I’d like to admit.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or blame yourself excessively when these thoughts about missing something important come up?\n\nPatient: Yeah. More than I probably should.\n\nIt’s like… if there’s one setting I missed, or one access rule I didn’t catch, then I feel like whatever happens is on me. Even if part of me knows that’s not really reasonable, it still lands that way. Like I should’ve prevented it.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience significant anxiety when you're not having these obsessive thoughts about missing something important?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure.\n\nI don’t think I’m anxious all the time for no reason… it’s more like I notice it when that specific worry is there. When it’s not, I guess I’m just kind of tense, but I haven’t really paid attention to it.\n\nDoctor: Are your obsessive thoughts usually about one specific type of mistake or problem, or do they cover a wide range of possible mistakes or problems?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly one kind of thing, I guess.\n\nMissing something important — like one setting, one access rule, some warning sign — and then… someone gets hurt, or loses everything. It branches out a little, but it usually comes back to that. Like if I miss it, it’s on me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about missing something important are in line with your own values and beliefs?\n\nPatient: Not really. When it’s not, I guess I’m just kind of tense, but I haven’t really paid attention to it.\n\nDoctor: Are your obsessive thoughts usually about one specific type of mistake or problem, or do they cover a wide range of possible mistakes or problems?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly one kind of thing, I guess.\n\nMissing something important — like one setting, one access rule, some warning sign — and then… someone gets hurt, or loses everything. It branches out a little, but it usually comes back to that. Like if I miss it, it’s on me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about missing something important are in line with your own values and beliefs?\n\nPatient: Not really. I mean… my values are to be careful, to protect things, do the job right.\n\nBut the thoughts go way past that. Part of me knows it doesn’t make sense that one missed setting means I’m personally responsible for everything that could happen. It just… feels that way in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about missing something important are something you want to have or are they unwelcome to you?\n\nPatient: They’re definitely not thoughts I want.\n\nThey’re… intrusive, I guess. I don’t sit there trying to think about all the ways I could miss something. It just hits me — like if I overlook one setting or warning sign, then whatever happens is on me. I know part of me doesn’t really believe it that way, but it still feels hard to ignore."
